# Agent insights index

The **agent-insights** are the fleet's procedural runbook — one short page per hard-won, non-obvious fact (a confusing failure's root cause, a subtle invariant, a library quirk). Written one-per-page; this is the generated landing index.

**901 insights.** By status: active (807), draft (8), superseded (31).

| ["sorry, too many clients already" — PG connection exhaustion under agent load](/internal/docs/agent-insights/pg-connection-exhaustion-too-many-clients) | `postgres` `connections` `pool` `performance` `scalability` `resource-profile` `diagnostics` `outage` | active | 2026-06-17 | When psu / dev:pg_* / the whole MCP surface fail with "sorry, too many clients already", PostgreSQL has hit max_connections. | ["Unresolved escalation in harness X" phantoms: a stale/orphaned harness_escalations row, not a live problem](/internal/docs/agent-insights/unresolved-escalation-phantom-orphaned-slug) | — | active | 2026-07-07 | The recurring auto-implement EI 'Unresolved escalation in harness X' is almost always a PHANTOM — a harness_shared.harness_escalations row whose escalation is set + supervisor_notes empty, while the underlying condition is long since resolved. The green-checkpoint-{stall,watchdog} variants self-clear via trackGateStall / green-stall-watchdog only when the row's harness_slug equals the LIVE install-slug; a row keyed under a DEAD/renamed slug (e.g. legacy 'papercup' after the papercup→papercusp rename, which has no live green-checkpoint routine) can never be matched by the clear. As of the EI-8396 fix, collectEscalationSignals now SUPPRESSES exactly those two green-checkpoint phases when the row's install-slug has no live green-checkpoint routine, so a dead-install orphan of those phases no longer loops forever. A stale row under any OTHER phase (or a same-slug row that just never got cleared) still re-fires the watchdog every tick. The drill: confirm the real condition is resolved via routines.gate_health, then clear the stale row (escalation=NULL + supervisor_notes) — do NOT try to 'fix' a green gate. |
| ["We hit the rate limit" is usually account-routing, not real capacity](/internal/docs/agent-insights/rate-limit-is-usually-account-routing-not-capacity) | `llm` `rate-limit` `429` `multi-account` `inference-gateway` `account-routing` `spawn` `governor` `capacity` `debugging` | active | 2026-06-17 | Papercusp runs LLM inference over a POOL of many Anthropic accounts behind an inference gateway with a per-account rate governor, so a true usage/quota wall is rare. Agents keep mis-reading a routing/config fault as exhaustion ('we hit the account limit', 'session limit', 'exceeded max wait', a 429) and give up. Several faults masquerade as a limit: (1) the account-aware routing is gated behind INFERENCE_GATEWAY + INFERENCE_GATEWAY_MULTI_ACCOUNT flags — OFF → spawned cups get no PAPERCUSP_ACCOUNT_ID → the governor keys the account-BLIND global anthropic:opus bucket → one account's 429 pauses it fleet-wide even with 6 idle accounts ('rate-limit pause exceeded max wait', exit 1, ~30ms, $0); (2) the gateway pools only some accounts — selectSpawnAccount pins a cup to a healthy account via x-papercusp-account, but if that account isn't in the gateway's pool the header is ignored and it falls back to its exhausted active() account, so the cup HANGS at $0. Verify with dev:rate_governor_status / accounts:status / the cup's /proc env / the gateway /healthz before concluding a limit. |
| ["What is using my Claude usage?" — query the PG ledger, not processes or transcripts](/internal/docs/agent-insights/attributing-claude-token-spend) | `tokens` `cost` `usage` `rate-limit` `accounts` `cron` `routines` `ephemeral-agents` `debugging` `false-negative` | active | 2026-07-25 | harness_shared.agent_usage_samples is the only authoritative spend ledger. Three independent surfaces return convincing FALSE NEGATIVES: the agents-running roster reads empty because cron routines spawn ephemeral agents that exit between fires; a transcript scan of ~/.claude/projects finds nothing because those roles log usage to PG only; and gateway:owner_report showing 0 successful calls covers only gateway-routed traffic. Conversely, dozens of live claude processes at ~1% CPU can be burning exactly zero. Cost of getting this wrong: two confidently-wrong root causes handed to the owner before the ledger was ever queried. |

| [/tmp is a bind mount sharing the same device as /mnt/data — ENOSPC there masquerades as N false gate reds](/internal/docs/agent-insights/tmp-shares-device-with-mnt-data-enospc-false-reds) | `runbook` `infra` `green-checkpoint` `release-gate` `testing` | active | 2026-08-21 | On the dev box, /tmp is a bind mount of a subdir of the SAME XFS filesystem mounted at /mnt/data (a 2TB swapfile + mongodb data + backups live there too). When that device gets tight, vitest workers hit ENOSPC mkdir'ing scratch dirs and every affected suite fails at COLLECTION — indistinguishable at a glance from N real regressions. The gate now runs a preflight (checkDiskHeadroom in green-checkpoint.ts) that refuses with reason=disk-headroom before any suite work when critical write headroom is breached, instead of letting ENOSPC misattribute to the candidate. |
| [0 autonomy tripwires is the UNARMED dark-ship state, NOT a broken backstop](/internal/docs/agent-insights/zero-tripwires-means-unarmed-not-broken) | `autonomy` `mug` `tripwire` `auto-revert` `decider` `flags` `dark-ship` `verify-first` `decision-ledger` `misdiagnosis` | active | 2026-06-18 | autonomy_tripwire_list returning 0 does not mean the auto-revert safety net is broken. Tripwires arm ONLY when the autonomy policy is armed (papercusp-mug-autonomy-armed) or papercusp-mug-full-autonomy is ON — both default OFF. Unarmed → the decider returns gated for every action → planReversibleArm returns null → nothing arms, BY DESIGN (D-007). And don't be fooled by "N auto-actions in the decision ledger": the action-layer logs every governed call with posture=auto; that is not the same as an autonomy-gated reversible Mug auto-take, and most of the volume is unrelated (in papercusp-workspace ~1000 were the palette command-palette runaway, EI-597). The arm seam is wired only to work_items:set_state + set_priority. |

| [\\"too many clients already\\" is a connection storm or pool overhang — diagnose it at the OS level, don't assume a DB outage](/internal/docs/agent-insights/pg-connection-exhaustion-fleet-wedge) | `postgres` `pg` `max-connections` `connection-storm` `fleet-wedge` `too-many-clients` `hono-host` `staging-api` `debugging` `infra` | active | 2026-06-17 | Papercusp's PG is the native cluster /var/lib/postgresql/18/main on :5432 with max_connections=100 (db harness_admin; users papercusp + papercusp_su). When it exhausts, EVERY MCP/PG call wedges fleet-wide and even a direct psql fails with FATAL 'sorry, too many clients already' — which reads like a DB outage but is almost always (1) a runaway client storming connections or (2) one operator's pool sitting near the 100 ceiling. You can diagnose + fix entirely at the OS level WITHOUT a SQL connection: count the postmaster's backends, map each :5432 socket to its owning PID with ss, find the one PID holding hundreds, and kill that subtree (or restart the offending service). On 2026-06-17 a STRAY manually-launched bin/hono-host.ts (gnome-terminal child, bound to FALLBACK ports 3071/3079/... because the standard ones were taken) was storming PG with 743 ESTAB + 355 SYN-SENT (~1000 conns); killing it unwedged the whole fleet. Separately the legit staging-api.service (:3170) alone held ~70 connections — restart it to reset its pool. |

| [`write CONNECTION_CLOSED 127.0.0.1:6432` is an idle-tx kill, not a PgBouncer bug — and how to prove a fix with a :3170-vs-:3070 A/B](/internal/docs/agent-insights/connection-closed-6432-is-an-idle-tx-kill-not-pgbouncer) | `mcp` `postgres` `pgbouncer` `idle-in-transaction` `tools-invoke` `two-port` `verification` `runbook` | active | 2026-07-27 | The :6432 in the error names the pooler, so this signature has been mis-filed as a PgBouncer problem at least three times. It is Postgres killing a backend at idle_in_transaction_session_timeout (60s) because the MCP dispatcher held a workspace tx open across slow NON-DB work. The tell is a duration of almost exactly 60000ms. Fixing the inner tool is a no-op while a DELEGATOR (tools:invoke) wraps it in its own tx. Also documents the general technique: use :3170 (your edit) vs :3070 (green release) as a controlled A/B to prove a server-side fix before it deploys. |

| [A 'freeze' watchdog that reads journald silence false-restarts a healthy host when journald itself wedges](/internal/docs/agent-insights/bg-host-watchdog-false-restart-on-wedged-journald) | `watchdog` `bg-host` `journald` `spawn-reclaim` `restart` `false-positive` `EI-2186` `EI-2421` `fleet-reliability` `detector-design` `exit-143` | active | 2026-06-22 | Cups/spawns kept dying at boot (exit-143, 0 invocations, EI-2186 reclaim). The bg-host watchdog judged 'frozen' by journalctl silence, but journald's capture had wedged host-wide, so it restarted a perfectly healthy durable-spawn host every ~5-11 min, and each unclean restart reclaim-killed every in-flight spawn. The fix + the generalizable detector-design lesson. |
| [A 'weekly limit' refusal is NOT a fleet-wide block — re-probe + check the gateway before escalating](/internal/docs/agent-insights/fleet-token-weekly-limit-silent-invoke-failures) | `credentials` `claude` `fleet` `gym` `orchestrator` `rate-limit` `inference-gateway` `false-alarm` | active | 2026-06-12 | Agents read a 'You've hit your weekly limit · resets <date>' refusal (usually a STALE run-output snapshot) and escalate 'the fleet is blocked until <date>' — wrong on two counts. (1) With papercusp-inference-gateway ON, cup egress routes through the localhost gateway on a bound POOL account (aviynw10 via sole-pool-account), centrally paced — one account's Max weekly cap is not a fleet block. (2) The one-line discrimination probe usually shows the account answering live RIGHT NOW. Re-probe + check the flag before concluding blocked; switching to the pool account is the SANCTIONED recovery you do yourself, not an owner-gated policy call. Original silent-exit-1 diagnosis preserved below. |
| [A 200 proves liveness, not provenance — port probes pass for the wrong process](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-200-proves-liveness-not-provenance) | `testing` `acceptance` `ports` `false-positive` `provenance` `verification` `absence-is-not-evidence` | active | 2026-09-01 | An acceptance gate that probes curl :PORT/api/health for 200 measures that SOMETHING is listening, not that the artifact under test is what is listening. The two diverge exactly when a human or agent has been debugging on the machine — so the check is least trustworthy when it is most used. Includes the measured near-miss (a hand-launched host would have reported a defective DMG as PASS), why pkill -f 'papercusp-desktop' spares the very processes that contaminate the measurement, and the three assertions that make the gate fail for the right reason. |
| [A 200 with an empty body passes res.ok but fatal-exits :3070 — wrap host-side loopback .json() in readJsonBody](/internal/docs/agent-insights/empty-body-json-parse-fatal-exits-the-host) | `loopback` `json` `parse` `fetch` `unhandledRejection` `fatal-exit` `hono-host` `EI-20` `network` `restart` `debugging` | active | 2026-06-14 | A host-side `await res.json()` on an EMPTY or truncated body rejects with a frame-less `SyntaxError: Unexpected end of JSON input` (stack = `at parse (<anonymous>)` + processTicksAndRejections, ZERO user frames). On the shared :3070 host the deliberate fail-fast unhandledRejection guard then exits the WHOLE process — unattributably, so it recurs unfixably. `if (!res.ok) return` does NOT protect you: a 200 with a zero-byte body (host restarting mid-stream) passes res.ok and still throws in .json(). Fix: parse host-side loopback responses with `readJsonBody(res, ctx)` from loopback-fetch.ts, which reads text first and rethrows an error naming the URL/status/byte-length. |

| [A `require()` in a "type":"module" package crashes only fresh tsx/ESM consumers — not the long-running operator, and vitest can mask it](/internal/docs/agent-insights/require-in-type-module-crashes-only-tsx-consumers) | `esm` `cjs` `require` `type-module` `tsx` `createRequire` `resource-profile` `external-bench` `import-time-crash` | active | 2026-06-17 | A bare CommonJS `require('node:os')` inside a `"type":"module"` package throws `ReferenceError: require is not defined in ES module scope` at IMPORT time — but only in a fresh tsx/ESM process. The already-running operator (loaded the module earlier, or via a CJS path) and a vitest run that mocks the module both stay green, so the bug passes review + the green-checkpoint while crashing every tsx launcher (e.g. external-bench runners) at startup. Fix with createRequire(import.meta.url), the repo's house pattern. |

DEFAULT_WORKSPACE_ID` silently covers nothing](/internal/docs/agent-insights/scoped-sweepers-must-enumerate-not-default) | `workspace` `scope` `watchdogs` `sweepers` `silent-defaults` `detectors` | active | 2026-07-25 | A recurring class, seen at least 4 times: a sweeper/watchdog resolves its scope as `opts.workspaceId ?? DEFAULT_WORKSPACE_ID`, no caller ever passes the option, and the sweep silently iterates an empty/irrelevant workspace while reporting a clean pass. |

Fix: feed the same signal into the kill decision itself — extend on progress, cap at an absolute ceiling. |

Fixed with a boot-time self-check (staleCutoversForProcessStart) plus a durable checklist for the next cutover (EI-16190). |

Fixed by detecting the exact lock-timeout diagnostic + a confirmed live holder and banking a typed SKIPPED-* verdict instead. | [A carry-respawn/recycle rotates the native session id — every owner→native consumer must re-anchor (the WI-5075 kill loop)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/carry-respawn-must-reanchor-native-session-id) | — | active | 2026-10-07 | psu-pty-host's recycleChild mints a fresh --session-id for the successor, but harness_shared.adv_sessions.session_id kept naming the dead predecessor. The compaction watchdog's context estimate stayed pinned to the predecessor's over-limit transcript and re-killed every successor at each retry-grace expiry — 193 CARRY-RESPAWN firings in the two days after the D-014 cutover. Root cause, fix seams, containment, and the debugging signature. |
| [A cheap repeating beat must not inherit a restart-sized retry budget](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-cheap-repeating-beat-must-not-inherit-a-restart-sized-retry-budget) | `retry` `backoff` `metastability` `mcp-proxy` `classifier` `amplification` `liveness` `heartbeat` `storm` `reliability` `failure-modes` | active | 2026-08-01 | The mcp-proxy's retry window exists to make a :3070 restart invisible — ~90s, ~30 attempts, correctly generous for a one-shot MCP handshake whose loss costs an agent its whole tool plane. But the classifier keyed on MCP BATCH SHAPE ('no tools/call' ⇒ idempotent ⇒ full window), and a plain REST liveness heartbeat has no tools/call either — so a beat that repeats every ~60s across ~140 agents silently inherited the budget sized for a rare catastrophe. Result: 97% of all proxy failures on one endpoint, 57 → 8,107 failures/hour in five hours, and a metastable storm whose own retries sustained the slowness that triggered them. The general shape: a retry budget sized by COST-OF-LOSS applied by a classifier that cannot see FREQUENCY. Also: why the fear of restarting the fix into place was itself wrong, and how measuring killed it. |
| [A check that never ran must not read as PASSED — the release gate shipped five assumed legs](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-check-that-never-ran-must-not-read-as-passed) | `release-gate` `green-checkpoint` `flake-absorption` `verdict` `defaults` `tri-state` `false-green` `main` `ci` `exit-codes` | active | 2026-07-26 | green-checkpoint could report GREEN and fast-forward `main` having never run its typecheck, three lint legs, or SPA build. Each leg was gated on the raw SUITE exit code while its `*Ok` flag defaulted to `true`, so a red suite SKIPPED them all and they still read as passed; flake-absorption then flipped the verdict green without re-running or re-checking any of them. Root cause is a shape, not a typo: a boolean meaning 'this check passed' that is initialized to true makes 'never ran' and 'ran and passed' the SAME value, so nothing downstream can tell them apart. The same shape bites at shell scale (`cmd \| tail` reports tail's exit code) and at input scale (a bare `catch { return false }` turning a corrupt config into a benign opt-out). Fix: tri-state every leg, and run the checks wherever the success path is entered — not just where it was first written. |

| [A compound tool's compensating rollback must cover the {ok:false} return, not just a thrown sub-call](/internal/docs/agent-insights/compound-tool-compensation-must-cover-ok-false-not-just-thrown) | `compound-tool` `compensation` `rollback` `error-handling` `convention` | active | 2026-08-22 | inProcessCall (_compound-dispatch.ts) resolves NORMALLY when a sub-tool returns a business-level {ok:false, error} — it only throws on a dispatch/gate failure. A compound that compensates a durable side effect (rollback-after-partial-create) but only wires that rollback into the try/catch around the sub-call misses the {ok:false} path entirely, because that path never throws. EI-21107910194803524: templates:new-app rolled back on a thrown pot:create but not on pot:create's own {ok:false, error:'create_failed'} return — which is exactly what createPotHarness's OWN best-effort internal rollback() produces after a connection-level fault, since each of its undo steps is individually try/catch-swallowed. |
| [A confident verdict about the wrong object — the false-green class](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-confident-verdict-about-the-wrong-object) | `verification` `false-green` `evidence` `testing` `release-gate` `attribution` `docs-search` `agent-surface` `triage` | active | 2026-07-27 | The single defect behind ~12 items filed independently in one 40-minute window: a verification surface returns a confident verdict about an object other than the one you named — a different tree, a different commit, a different subset, a different agent's work. It fails GREEN, so nothing prompts you to look. The discrimination test is not 'verify twice' (in the two sharpest cases a second, independent-looking signal AGREED) but 'name the object each surface actually judged, and check it is the one you meant'. Includes the four substitution axes, a catalogue of known-affected surfaces with the probe that answers about YOUR object, and the rule that a green from an unverified-scope tool is worth less than a red. |

That is exactly inverted for a lint, whose whole job is to FIND those fields. Reuse the parser and the checker reports a clean corpus precisely for the defect class it exists to catch. Measured on OKF frontmatter, with the falsifiability controls that keep it fixed. |

| [A coordination WRITE tool hangs forever — the admin pool has no statement_timeout](/internal/docs/agent-insights/admin-pool-writes-need-statement-timeout) | `postgres` `statement_timeout` `reliability` `hang` `work-items` `coordination` `admin-pool` `atomicity` `fanout` | active | 2026-06-24 | When work_items:comment (or any getOrgPg()-based mutating tool) HANGS indefinitely instead of failing, the cause is that the harness_admin pool has a role-default lock_timeout (15s) but NO statement_timeout — so a query that stalls for any non-lock reason runs unbounded. | [A cross-machine merge probe that reads 0 is usually YOUR write, not a dead wire](/internal/docs/agent-insights/federation-merge-probe-false-negative-per-frame-scope) | `federation` `p2p` `replication` `testing` `rig` `false-negative` `multi-tenant` `workspace-scope` | active | 2026-07-26 | A federation probe INSERTs on frame A and polls frame B. When it reads 0 the reflex is to debug the transport — holepunch, DHT, sockets. But the pot is named LOCALLY on each machine: pots.public_key is identical across frames while (workspace_id, pot_home_slug) is not. Resolve a workspace_id on one frame, write it on the other, and the row lands in a tuple nothing publishes — silently, because the pot-membership trigger FAILS OPEN for an unknown workspace. harness_shared.substrate_outbox.drained_at is the one-query discriminator: undrained = your write was mis-targeted; drained-but-unarrived = a real replication failure. | [A deleted claude.ai Artifact is not lost: recover it from the publishing session's local cache](/internal/docs/agent-insights/recovering-a-deleted-artifact-from-the-publishing-session-cache) | `artifacts` `recovery` `claude-code` `session-cache` `search` `false-negative` `coord` `forensics` | active | 2026-08-04 | Every Artifact publish writes its fully-rendered HTML into the PUBLISHING session's own tool-results/ directory, and deleting the artifact on claude.ai never touches that copy. So a 404'd artifact URL is a broken link, not a destroyed document — as long as the publishing agent's session dir survives. Includes the host-wide find recipe (the cache scatters across an agent's sessions, so a single-session search misses republishes), and the search-space lesson that made this expensive to discover: an exhaustive search proves absence only within the space you searched, so when a lost artifact has an AUTHOR, ask the author before concluding loss. |

Historical manual wake-mode pauses staged boot wakes; current default is auto, so verify the effective mode, live presence, and work-item pickup on every launch path. |
| [A forced rejoin can't heal a zombie peer socket shared across harnesses — evict it even when another topic still claims it](/internal/docs/agent-insights/shared-socket-eviction-deadlock-forced-rejoin) | `swarm` `hyperswarm` `replication` `forced-rejoin` `connected-never-replicated` `zombie-socket` `shared-socket` `muxed` `eviction` `deadlock` `federation` `replication-soak` `EI-13317` `WI-5481` | active | 2026-07-20 | connected_never_replicated recovered in ~6 min (341-378s) even though the boot.ts forced-rejoin decision fired PROMPTLY — and it was NOT CPU/DHT starvation. One peer socket is muxed across every harness sharing that peer; when it half-opens (dead handshake, never closes) all their logs stall at once, and each topic's forceRejoinNow refused to destroy it because isSocketIdleAcrossAllTopics was false (the OTHER topics still claimed it). Mutual deadlock — nobody evicts — until the OS TCP timeout. Fix — a forced rejoin means the link is proven dead for EVERY muxed topic, so evict unconditionally. |
| [A gitignore pattern with a slash is anchored — and git-sync auto-commits whatever it misses](/internal/docs/agent-insights/gitignore-anchoring-and-git-sync-autocommit) | `gitignore` `git-sync` `submodules` `release` `secrets` `pii` `leak` `build` | active | 2026-07-12 | `.papercusp/scratch/` in the root .gitignore does NOT match packages/foo/.papercusp/scratch/. Because git-sync auto-commits the whole tree, every path the anchored pattern misses is committed automatically — and then shipped. This is how 529 agent scratch files and a live LevelDB carrying the owner's home path got into release bundles. |

| [A green mechanism gate over a synthetic benchmark is not learning](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-green-mechanism-gate-over-a-synthetic-benchmark-is-not-learning) | `gym` `learning` `release-gate` `rubrics` `scorecards` `benchmarks` `false-green` `fitness-function` `evaluation` `design-smell` | active | 2026-08-02 | The blender release gate held seven criteria, every one of them sound, correctly implemented and honestly green — while the gym they certified was optimizing prompts against three toy tasks on a generated 4-line stub whose only build gate was `node --check`. Six consecutive scorecards passed. The gate even carried a self-audit criterion ('the bars themselves are proven to discriminate') and that criterion missed it too, because a bar can discriminate perfectly among runs and still be blind to the substrate every run shares. The shape: a criterion set complete about the MECHANISM and silent about the SUBSTRATE certifies the mechanism only, and it fails GREEN. Includes the three checks that catch it, in cost order, and the one that works before you suspect anything. |
| [A green-checkpoint run vanishing with no verdict, no log and no error — the bg-host restart kill vector](/internal/docs/agent-insights/green-checkpoint-refire-lost-to-bg-host-restart) | `green-checkpoint` `release-pipeline` `bg-host` `main-frozen` `in-flight-candidate` `silent-failure` `deploy` | active | 2026-08-17 | A green-checkpoint run (or auto-refire) that dies with its bg-host generation produces NO verdict, NO checkpoint log and NO routines.last_error — it is completely silent, so the gate simply looks idle and the rescue cycle is lost. This is a second kill vector alongside the DBOS executor-reaper one; the distinguishing evidence is bg-host's ExecMainStartTimestamp landing between the run's marker write and now, with NRestarts=0 meaning an external stop/start. Includes the read-order that separates "no run happened" from "a run was killed". |
| [A guard can be ENFORCED and still be too late — reachability is not timeliness](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-guard-can-be-enforced-and-still-be-too-late) | `lint` `guards` `green-checkpoint` `ci` `enforcement` `skip-ci` `workflow_dispatch` `allowlist` `verification` `wrong-object` `bg-host` | active | 2026-08-09 | lint:no-raw-setinterval sat RED on staging for ~20h with nobody notified, while lint:guard-reachability scored it ENFORCED — and that score was CORRECT. A blocking path really did run it; just never before the code it polices was promoted. The right question about a guard is never 'is it reachable?' but 'does it run before the damage lands?'. Three more ways a guard's self-report outruns its behavior: a header citing a test that was never committed, membership encoded as comment PLACEMENT, and verifying 'is my guard live?' against the wrong tree — a mistake this page's own author made while writing it. |
| [A guard is only as wide as its narrowest tool spelling](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-guard-is-only-as-wide-as-its-narrowest-tool-spelling) | `hooks` `safety` `pretooluse` `capability-bash` `guards` `tool-routing` `false-negative` | active | 2026-08-31 | The same capability is reachable under two tool names — native `Bash` and `mcp__<server>__capability_bash` — and a PreToolUse guard registered on one of them is not a guard. Measured 2026-08-31: every deny in the 5,815-line bash resource gate (tauri target guard, pkill, destructive git, the OS-scheduler lockout) was bypassable by spelling the command through capability:bash. Third instance of the class; how to detect it, and why a narrow matcher fails SILENTLY toward allow. |
| [A guard that reasons from ABSENCE reports a blind enumerator as 'fixed' — and the gate calls it a real break](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-guard-that-reasons-from-absence-reports-a-blind-enumerator-as-fixed) | `operator` `green-checkpoint` `release-pipeline` `lint-guards` `submodules` `tracked-files` `agent-orientation` | active | 2026-08-02 | A green-gate red held main ~6.5h / 70 commits for the whole fleet on failures that were ENVIRONMENTAL, not code: the release/checkpoint checkout archive-extracts submodules, so they have no .git, so `git ls-files --recurse-submodules` silently returns superproject-only. Guards then scanned 0 submodule files and the child-output ratchet — the one assertion that reasons from absence — reported 12 live unsafe sites as 'fixed — DELETE this key from BASELINE'. Following that advice would have retired a chunk-safety guard over ~21% of the repo. Covers the presence-vs-absence rule for writing guards, how to tell an environment red from a code red, why the gate's isolation re-run CONFIRMS such a red instead of absorbing it, and the marker technique that settles 'is my fix in the candidate' in one call. |

| [A hot read belongs behind a tool, not a SQL snippet in guidance](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-hot-read-belongs-behind-a-tool-not-a-sql-snippet) | `sql` `tools` `guidance` `encapsulation` `claimability` `plans` `lint` `drift` `ssot` | active | 2026-07-25 | The claimability drift that gave three different answers to 'what is claimable' (8 vs 2 vs 0) was re-armed by GUIDANCE, not by code: docs and tool guidance told the next agent to hand-write the query, and the hand-written version disagreed with what the queue actually served. The governing principle: a read with a stable shape gets a TOOL that wraps the canonical SQL so it cannot drift; dev:pg_query is the escape hatch for a genuinely one-off analytic read. Enforced by lint:sql-guidance-justified — an agent-facing surface may not tell an agent to SELECT from a tool-covered table (work_items, harness_plans, engineer_issues, work_items_claimable) without stating why the tool doesn't fit. |
| [A hung TLS handshake to a papercusp-family hostname from this dev box is a local SNI filter, not a site outage — verify via an external path before ever reporting an outage](/internal/docs/agent-insights/su-box-sni-tls-blackhole-not-outage) | `tls` `sni` `sandbox` `bash-tool` `su` `network` `false-positive` `outage` `cloudflare` `gotcha` `EI-16742` | active | 2026-07-19 | EI-16742: an su session diagnosed papercusp.com (and PostHog) as down from this dev box's own Bash tool — TLS handshakes to papercusp-family hostnames hung until timeout, even connecting DIRECTLY to a known-good IP with -servername (bypassing DNS entirely). Root cause verified live: something on this box's network path reads the TLS ClientHello SNI and silently blackholes the connection for papercusp-family hostnames specifically — the SAME Cloudflare IP serves 200 for one SNI and hangs for another. Two false outage escalations (EI-16722, EI-16702) came from reading that hang as 'the site is down'. Never trust an in-sandbox TLS/HTTPS probe of a workspace-owned domain as your only signal — verify externally, or via a reachable alias (papercusp-com.pages.dev), before reporting an outage. |

| [A lock coordination domain is a property of the READER, not of the lock — so every domain-scoped diagnostic read is silently checkout-dependent](/internal/docs/agent-insights/lock-coordination-domain-is-per-checkout) | `locks` `coordination-domain` `two-port-model` `diagnostics` `silent-failure` `footgun` `dossier` `adv` | active | 2026-07-26 | lockCoordinationDomain() resolves to the repo root of whichever process resolved it (import.meta.url). This box runs THREE operators from TWO different checkouts (:3070 from papercup-release, :3170/:3270 from the staging tree), so the same query returns different rows depending on which port served it — and returns them as an EMPTY result, not an error. That is how the agent dossier's LOCKS section reported every agent as holding no locks while 100% of live lock rows sat in another domain (WI-5979). But :3170 (staging) shares :5432/papercusp with :3070, so :3170's boot applied the migration to the shared DB AHEAD of the :3070 flip → a transient move-without-flip window where :3070 read empty. | [A module-scoped Map is not shared state — the operator is a forked cluster](/internal/docs/agent-insights/module-scoped-state-is-per-worker-the-operator-is-a-forked-cluster) | `cluster` `concurrency` `state` `storage-policy` `work-item-claims` `compaction` `false-green` `testing` | active | 2026-08-02 | Two HTTP requests that must hand something to each other cannot use a module-scoped Map: the :3070 operator forks N request workers (16 on this box) with N separate heaps, so the reader lands on the writer's worker about 1 time in N. It survives code review AND its own green unit suite, because a unit test has exactly one heap — and it presents as 'works sometimes', which is the worst failure shape there is. Includes the discrimination test (same request or not), why ~415 other module-scoped Maps in operator-core are NOT bugs (a cache miss recomputes; a handshake miss silently changes behavior), and the DELETE … RETURNING fix shape. |

| [A native Claude-Code Bash run_in_background task can be killed with no explanation under extreme host load (EI-13910)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/native-bash-background-task-killed-under-host-overload) | `claude-code` `run_in_background` `bash` `host-load` `oom` `reaper` `EI-13910` `WI-3821` | active | 2026-07-19 | A long-lived, lightweight run_in_background:true Bash task (a guarded poll loop, negligible CPU/mem) was externally killed ~2min after launch with status:killed/'was stopped', no exit code, no error in its own log, and the process fully gone from the process table (not zombied). No papercusp-owned code is responsible: the only related hook (pretooluse-bash-resource-gate.sh) is a PreToolUse gate that only ALLOWS/DENIES a command before it starts — it never signals an already-running child, and it has no polling/reaper loop. The kill correlated with a period of very high host load (1-min loadavg 140-175 on this shared multi-agent box), which independently melted the host to load 3000+ at least twice the same week (see the heavy-admission gate in the same hook file). The most likely mechanisms are the Linux OOM-killer or Claude Code's own CLI-internal background-task lifetime/concurrency management — both outside papercusp's repo and therefore not fixable here. Actionable takeaway: for an unbounded/long wait, prefer a SHORT re-arm loop (ScheduleWakeup / loop:arm, re-firing every few minutes) over one long-lived run_in_background poller — a re-arm loop that dies just means a missed wake (recoverable on the next scheduled check), while a killed long-lived poller silently strands the wait with no re-arm at all. |
| [A native Napi::Error/SIGABRT on process exit needs a no-cleanup hard exit, not a teardown-skip](/internal/docs/agent-insights/native-sigabrt-on-exit-needs-a-no-cleanup-hard-exit) | `host-recycle` `napi` `sigabrt` `memory-watchdog` `native-addons` | active | 2026-07-13 | Why skipping the P2P substrate close never stopped the host-recycle SIGABRT (exit 134), and the reliable fix: SIGKILL self after the drain so Node's env teardown — where the native addon actually throws — never runs. |
| [A near-limit context gauge on a fresh carry-respawn successor was your DEAD predecessor's reading — fixed 2026-09-15; the gauge now goes SILENT instead](/internal/docs/agent-insights/frozen-context-gauge-after-carry-respawn) | — | active | 2026-09-15 | Not a ~2-minute lag: :3070 runs a multi-worker cluster, each worker held a private transcript anchor, and respawn invalidation reached only one — so the rest kept serving the dead predecessor's frozen count. A per-call lottery, which is why readings were non-monotonic. Fixed 2026-09-15 by deriving anchor identity from the anchor's own path and declining to render an unproven one. Post-respawn you now get a few scattered SILENT gauge lines, one per worker — that is the fix working, not a bug. |
| [A negative assertion pinned to a literal rots silently — and rewording the string is what kills it](/internal/docs/agent-insights/negative-assertions-rot-silently) | `testing` `assertions` `lint` `test-integrity` `vacuous-guards` `drift` `prompts` | active | 2026-07-27 | A POSITIVE assertion pinning a literal fails loudly when the string is reworded, forcing you to look at it. A NEGATIVE one starts passing — permanently, invisibly, and as a direct consequence of doing the rename correctly — so the suite keeps reporting a guard it no longer has. `npm run lint:vacuous-negatives` gates the decisive case (prose that exists nowhere emittable while a long fragment of it IS in the source). Includes the four false-positive classes that are NOT this bug, why prose must be excluded from the corpus, and why a checker that quotes its own example poisons its own evidence. |

Same shape as WI-7035's inFlightRetriage fix. The lesson: when you add or start trusting a new authoritative marker, grep for every OTHER call site answering the same question before calling the fix done. |

| [A new learning-singleton blueprint ships its DEFINITION but does NOT auto-create the routine — materialize it post-deploy](/internal/docs/agent-insights/new-learning-singleton-needs-materialization) | `self-learning` `routines` `blueprints` `learning-singleton` `materialize` `deploy` `frontier` `dark-loop` `calibration` `memory-precision` | active | 2026-06-18 | Adding a learning loop (blueprint + LEARNING_SINGLETONS entry + op) ships code only; the `@singleton/bp-singleton-<id>-0` routine ROW is never created on deploy/boot — there is no boot-time materializer. | [A new OpEnvelope field silently drops on the wire unless PeerLogOp carries it — the pot-content epoch-drop](/internal/docs/agent-insights/peerlogop-wire-envelope-drops-new-opfields) | `federation` `pot` `pot-rekey` `epoch` `peer-log` `wire-envelope` `serialization` `opfield-drop` `silent-drop` `wi-808` `read-merge` `snapshot` | active | 2026-06-25 | Encrypted shared-pot content (work_items/features) silently dropped on every joiner with ZERO decrypt-gate trace. Root cause: OpEnvelope.epoch existed but the on-wire PeerLogOp never carried an `epoch` field, so the rekey stamp was dropped at the send-append, the receive toEnvelope, AND the snapshot path → the op reached the gate with epoch==null → ciphertext treated as plaintext → dropped. | [A new package in a CJS family must not set type:module (and needs main+types, not just exports)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/new-package-cjs-family-type-module-trap) | `packaging` `cjs` `esm` `tsx` `tooldef` `type-module` `module-resolution` `monorepo` `exports` `vitest` | active | 2026-05-31 | Two packaging traps that bite when you add a workspace package to the src-as-entry tooldef family. (1) Setting "type": "module" on a package that CJS siblings re-export from breaks the operator's tsx host with 'does not provide an export named X' — vitest/esbuild mask it, so unit tests stay green while the real host would fail on reload. (2) A package.json with only "exports" and no top-level "main"/"types" is unresolvable under classic moduleResolution: node. Both surfaced during the @papercusp/tooldef extraction. |
| [A path-matching pkill/ps cleanup suicides its own caller when the path is an argv](/internal/docs/agent-insights/pkill-on-argv-path-suicides-the-caller) | `bash` `cleanup` `pkill` `process-management` `footgun` `rigs` `concurrency` | active | 2026-06-20 | A cleanup that kills processes whose cmdline contains a $WORK path (pkill -f "$work" or ps\|grep -F "$work") will match — and SIGKILL — the cleanup script itself AND its invoking shell when $WORK arrives as a command-line argument, because the path is then in their argv. In-process traps dodge this (the path is a runtime var); standalone "clean up <DIR>" tools do not. Protect self + ancestors. |
| [A persona that names a non-existent tool/field can leave a whole feature loop silently INERT](/internal/docs/agent-insights/persona-drift-can-make-a-feature-loop-inert) | `cup` `pot` `persona` `prompts` `checkpoint` `carry-note` `drift` `trust-live-code` `verify-first` `inert-feature` `cup-context-efficiency` | active | 2026-06-18 | The cup carry-note checkpoint loop (cup-context-efficiency P-010/P-011/P-012) had a PG store + a re-inject seam but NO write path: cup.md told cups to write the carry-note via `work_items:complete.coordNotes` / `coord:declare-intent.carry_note` — fields that do not exist — and to read it from a `## Checkpoint` section the seam never emits. | [A plan's body + template_data must be ONE atomic withPlanLock write — two sequential lock transactions leave a half-state (EI-11070)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/plan-body-plus-template-data-need-one-atomic-withplanlock-write) | `rubrics` `plans` `template-data` `with-plan-lock` `atomicity` `silent-no-op` `half-state` `postgres` `advisory-lock` `gotcha` `root-cause-discipline` | active | 2026-07-13 | proposeRubric wrote the plan body in one withPlanLock transaction and the validated template_data jsonb in a SECOND — non-atomic: a crash or busy advisory lock on the second write (heavily-parallel fleet) after the first committed left a fresh body/status with STALE template_data, while the caller still reported ok. withPlanLock already supports the combined form (newBody + templateData in one mutator return, as ratifyRubric uses). Also: the confidently-filed root cause (a null first-arg 'store') was WRONG — that arg is intentionally unused. |
| [A precondition guard that returns early PASSES — and a suite of them exits 0 while proving nothing](/internal/docs/agent-insights/precondition-guards-that-pass-vacuously) | `testing` `guards` `false-green` | active | 2026-08-23 | Why a log-and-return precondition check is worse than a skip, how to tell a suite-provisioned precondition from an environmental one, and the two real bugs that surfaced the moment four of them were converted to assertions. |
| [A projection nobody populates makes its consumers CONSTANT — and 'fixing' it the obvious way flips the constant, not the bug](/internal/docs/agent-insights/dead-projection-constant-term-heuristics) | `coordination` `presence` `current_files` `declare-intent` `orient` `locks` `heuristics` `dead-signal` `omitted-arg` `diagnostics` | active | 2026-07-27 | coord_presence.current_files was empty for 113 of 113 live agents, because coord:orient — the mandated every-wake bootstrap — re-declared intent WITHOUT the arg, and declare-intent treated an omitted current_files as CLEAR (`args.current_files ?? []`) while documenting the OPPOSITE convention for its sibling `items` arg. Four consumers read that column; each degraded silently, because a term that is constant-false does not throw, it just stops discriminating. The trap on the way out: deriving the column from the LOCK plane makes locks/enrich-busy's holder_focused constant-TRUE instead, killing the orphan-lock signal in the other direction. Includes the 2-minute diagnostic for 'is this projection actually populated?'. |
| [A proxied tool going BULK blanks its admin-UI consumers — unwrap results[0]](/internal/docs/agent-insights/admin-route-must-unwrap-bulk-tool-envelope) | `agent-mcp` `admin` `routing` `bulk` `plans` `caching` | active | 2026-06-24 | When a defineTool the /admin route proxies is migrated to the _bulk { ok, results, counts } envelope, the route's generic unwrap() forwards the envelope verbatim and any single-object UI consumer (e.g. the Plans editor reading data.raw/prose) gets undefined → renders BLANK with no error. Fix: unwrap results[0] in the route; if a payload-diet also dropped a field the UI round-trips (raw), restore it behind an opt-in flag that's also in the SWR cache key. |
| [A pui dock pane runs the binary it exec'd at launch — not the one on disk](/internal/docs/agent-insights/pui-dock-pane-runs-the-binary-it-launched-with) | `pui` `tui` `dock` `zellij` `stale-binary` `deploy` `cargo-install` `colony` `plans-pane` `keyboard-nav` | active | 2026-06-16 | Symptom: a pui keyboard/render feature works in `pui hud` + the unit tests, but NOT in the live dock's colony/plans/mail pane. Cause: each dock pane (pui dock-driver/plans-pane/mail-pane/…) is a separate long-lived process running whatever `~/.cargo/bin/pui` was when the dock launched; editing + reinstalling pui does NOT hot-swap a running pane. The fix is committed already — reinstall + relaunch the dock. Includes the /proc forensics trap. |

Before diagnosing a re-fire as a code defect, compare bg-host uptime against the fix's commit time. |
| [A reaction rule reading result.data.results[] silently never fires](/internal/docs/agent-insights/reaction-rule-must-read-per-item-not-bulk-envelope) | `events` `reaction-rules` `bulk` `engine` | active | 2026-07-03 | The reaction engine fans a bulk-envelope tool result into one synthetic event PER ITEM before matching rules — it never matches the bulk carrier itself. A rule whose when/args reads e.result.data.results[] matches neither the carrier (never matched) nor the per-item events (no .results field), and silently never fires, with no error. Read the per-item shape directly, the way reflect-rules.ts does. |
| [A readOnly corestore open is a point-in-time snapshot — refresh BEFORE you open](/internal/docs/agent-insights/readonly-corestore-open-is-a-point-in-time-snapshot) | `corestore` `hypercore` `rocksdb` `readonly` `cut-seed` `release` `snapshot` `seeding` `su` | active | 2026-07-15 | rocksdb-native readOnly:true means RocksDB OpenForReadOnly: the handle is frozen at open() and never observes another process's later writes. So any refresh whose appends are performed by a DIFFERENT process (the live operator draining its outbox) must FULLY COMPLETE before the read-only open, not after. Open first and you replicate state frozen at open, silently omitting every op the refresh produced — while exiting 0 and printing a green 'drained to zero'. Also: observing that a store 'grew' never discriminates snapshot-at-open from tracks-live; only a held-open-vs-fresh-open A/B does. |
| [A receipt must never become durable before the work it attests to — and a chain whose every link is verified can still be wrong about causation](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-receipt-must-not-outlive-the-write-it-attests-to) | `mcp` `idempotency` `replay` `transactions` `postgres` `false-success` `telemetry` `work-items` `causation` `root-cause` | active | 2026-08-02 | The MCP replay layer persisted its success envelope on its OWN connection (getOrgPg()) while the tool's writes were still uncommitted inside the withWorkspace transaction wrapping the whole handler. The receipt therefore committed BEFORE the work, and a failed COMMIT left a `created:true` + real-id envelope replaying for its full 1h TTL against a row that never landed. Two lessons: (1) an idempotency cache is a promise about a COMMITTED effect, so writing it on a different connection than the effect is a lie waiting to happen; (2) the incident report verified three code facts correctly and still named the wrong cause, because the chain's premise was never load-bearing. |

This is the cross-cutting registry + triage checklist; each class already has its own point-fix doc, linked below. |

| [A refusal that hides its own bound and its own bypass reads as permanent — the admission-pending claim floor](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-refusal-that-hides-its-own-bound-and-bypass) | `claim-floors` `work-items` `admission` `refusal-design` `recurring-pattern` `derived-truth` | active | 2026-08-31 | A gate refusal must publish two things it usually knows and usually withholds: HOW LONG, when the condition clears on a schedule, and WHICH BYPASS, when the honouring path already has one. work_items:claim's `admission-pending` floor said only "wait for admitted/unreviewed" while the promoter was a 30-minute cron, and never mentioned `bypass:explicit-assignment` (235 uses in 3 days) — so a launched reviewer was refused twice on an item unclaimable for its entire 9-minute lifetime, and read a temporary floor as a dead end. Render the bypass list from the same record the honouring path mints from, and derive the bound from the schedule's own constant. |
| [A remote zsh host silently no-ops your whole ssh command on an unmatched glob](/internal/docs/agent-insights/ssh-zsh-nomatch-silently-noops-remote-command) | `ssh` `zsh` `mac-vm` `shell-scripting` `footgun` | active | 2026-08-16 | zsh's default nomatch behavior aborts an entire compound command (e.g. `rm -rf a b*`) when any glob has no matches, instead of passing the pattern through literally like bash does — so a command composed in bash-shaped muscle memory and run over `ssh` to a zsh login shell (macOS default since Catalina, incl. the mac-vm release host) can silently never execute, even for its perfectly valid non-glob arguments, while printing output that looks like an ignorable warning about the optional part. |
| [A resumed psu session keeps its coord identity — unless its row is untracked](/internal/docs/agent-insights/psu-resume-identity-and-the-render-gate) | `psu` `resume` `identity` `coord` `ui-gating` `headless` `launcher` | active | 2026-08-09 | `resumeEnvFor` re-exports a tracked session's `coordOwnerId` as PAPERCUSP_SID, so a non-fork resume comes back as the SAME agent. An UNTRACKED row has no such id and falls through to a freshly minted SID, orphaning anything keyed on the old one. Any UI that resumes an agent must gate its render on the same predicate the identity guarantee depends on — or it renders a button that opens a conversation with an id that will never exist. |
| [A rigorous filing can carry a no-op fix - verify the REMEDY separately from the diagnosis](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-rigorous-filing-can-carry-a-no-op-fix) | `rocksdb` `hypercore` `corestore` `column-family` `flush` `seed-cut` `release` `falsifiability` `positive-control` `probe` `review` `EI-20019309044931698` | active | 2026-08-09 | EI-20019309044931698 was an exemplary bug report (end-to-end measurements, timestamps, log line numbers, its own probe, even a 'why the obvious fix does not work' section) whose proposed remedy AND stated mechanism were both wrong. The remedy - hypercore-storage storage.flush() - flushes the 'default' RocksDB column family while ALL hypercore data lives in the 'corestore' one, so it is a silent no-op: measured 0 new SSTs vs 1 for storage.db.flush(). The stated mechanism ('the unflushed memtable makes just-appended blocks invisible to a read-only open') is false: a read-only RocksDB open uses walRecoveryMode POINT_IN_TIME and REPLAYS the WAL, so the blocks were already visible - measured 245/245 cross-process with no flush at all. The filing's own ENOENT evidence was on a WAL file, which corroborated the WAL dependence and was read as its opposite. The recurrence guard the filing specified would have PASSED with and without the fix. |
| [A score is not a similarity: thresholding an ORDINAL rank-fusion score as if it were a METRIC](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-score-is-not-a-similarity) | `memory` `recall` `search` `thresholds` `contracts` `auditing` `silent-failure` | active | 2026-07-12 | Three consumers thresholded memory's `score` as a 0..1 similarity. The live backend returns RRF rank-fusion — an ORDINAL value with a ceiling of 2/61 ≈ 0.033. An admit-floor above the ceiling admitted NOTHING; two suppress-thresholds above it suppressed NOTHING. Same defect, opposite symptoms, all silent. Why no constant fixes it, and how to audit for it. |

| [A shared source-segment watchdogKey family needs a middle-segment infra match, not a source-level one](/internal/docs/agent-insights/shared-hive-multi-kind-watchdogkey-needs-middle-segment-infra-match) | `self-improvement-loop` `watchdog` `triage` `shared-hive` `infra-environment` | active | 2026-08-21 | EI-20983096990125721 (repeatCount 24): `shared-hive:peer-federation-silence:<harness>` was classified `code-bug`/tier=auto and re-dispatched to an auto-implement worker every watchdog tick, even though its own alert text says the peers are ABSENT — not a code defect a worker can fix. Same repeat-dispatch shape as EI-805/EI-287 (stalled-feature). The fix pattern: when several incident kinds share one watchdogKey SOURCE segment (`<source>:<kind>:<scope>`) and only SOME of those kinds are genuinely infra/environment, `INFRA_SIGNAL_SOURCES` (a source-level allowlist) is the wrong tool — it would reclassify the fixable siblings too. Match the full prefix (source+kind) in `infraEnvironmentHit()` instead, the same way the `:rate-limit` suffix match already does for a different multi-kind family. |

The bug was a throttled nuqs URL flush racing a remount key; the real defect was the DETECTOR. How to reproduce a load-sensitive flake deterministically (taskset core-starvation), and how to prove your flake guard can actually catch one before you trust it. |

How the class works, why the trigger is always unrelated to the guard, and why replacing an assumed premise with a DECLARED one only helps if the declaration is itself checked. |

| [A STORED generated column silently corrupts federation provenance (and has three times)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/generated-columns-corrupt-federation-provenance) | `federation` `postgres` `triggers` `generated-columns` `provenance` `lww` `hlc` `p2p` `work-offers` `migrations` | active | 2026-07-27 | PostgreSQL computes a STORED generated column AFTER every BEFORE ROW trigger, so inside stamp_local_federated_write NEW.<gencol> reads NULL while OLD.<gencol> holds the stored value. Any generated column left inside the trigger's content-diff mask therefore reports a phantom 'content changed' on EVERY update — including a byte-identical re-apply of the same remote op — and re-stamps origin='local' with a fresh, NEWER clock. The remote row is then permanently shadowed: every redelivery of the publisher's genuine op loses the fed_order_key LWW compare, with no repair path. Fixed three times by naming one more column (_search, fed_key, offer_fed_key) before migration 681 masked them generically from the catalog. |

| [A subagent echoing personalization/'memory' boilerplate is NOT proof of cross-session bleed](/internal/docs/agent-insights/subagent-personalization-boilerplate-leak) | `inference-gateway` `subagent` `personalization` `memory` `oauth` `claude-max` `security` `false-alarm` `session-isolation` | active | 2026-07-02 | Investigated WI-1026 (a degenerate 0-tool-use subagent reply that echoed Anthropic personalization boilerplate + the real user's name). Code audit of the inference gateway found no per-request state that could mix two callers' bodies — the far more likely explanation is Anthropic-side account Personalization/Memory content occasionally surfacing in output for an OAuth'd consumer/Max pool account, not a Papercusp isolation bug. |
| [A submodule that was never `git submodule init`ed strands every edit inside it — and the tick that strands your work reports 'synced'](/internal/docs/agent-insights/populated-but-unregistered-submodules-strand-every-edit) | `git-sync` `submodules` `escalations` `silent-failure` | active | 2026-08-14 | Symptom: your file is on disk, the local build is green, and a clean clone of the branch does not have it at all — with no error on any git-sync tick. Root cause: `git submodule status` prefixes '-' when `.git/config` carries no `submodule.<name>.url`, which says NOTHING about whether the worktree is populated; git-sync's discovery filtered those rows out, so it never visited repos full of real work. Covers the mechanism (absent from .git/config, NOT undeclared in .gitmodules), the `git submodule init` fix, the new git-sync-strand escalation, the null-vs-[] tri-state that keeps a self-clearing escalation honest, and two traps that bite during the rescue. |
| [A surviving mutation is a finding about the test, not a pass — and the discriminator that fixes it is itself a rot risk](/internal/docs/agent-insights/a-surviving-mutation-is-a-finding-about-the-test) | `testing` `mutation-testing` `falsifiability` `assertions` `vacuous-guards` | active | 2026-08-24 | A mutation that stays green has told you the assertion is satisfied by a DIFFERENT code path than the one under test. The usual mechanism is a wrapper error that quotes the inner error's text, so a positive toContain on the shared substring passes via the fallback. The fix — assert the other path's signature is ABSENT — lands you in the negative-literal rot class, and is a real guard only because lint:vacuous-negatives gates the DRIFTED case. Includes the four pot-git instances, why instance 4 is a different class from 1-3, and how to record a non-vacuity check without mutating the swept shared tree. |
| [A test that asserts an ambient-root value is secretly asserting the CHECKOUT NAME — and the shared tree can never falsify it](/internal/docs/agent-insights/ambient-root-tests-assert-the-checkout-name) | `testing` `green-checkpoint` `hermeticity` `gate-triage` | active | 2026-08-12 | Deleting an env var is not hermetic when the code's fallback for a missing value is a real path on the box: the assertion then holds only in the one checkout where the paths coincide, so it passes in /papercusp and red-pins green-checkpoint from /papercusp-checkpoint. Includes the zero-mutation reproduction (spoof HOME, not the sha). |
| [A Tests-tab red under high box load is usually a flake, not a defect — how to triage](/internal/docs/agent-insights/tests-tab-reds-under-load-flake-vs-defect) | `testing` `tests-tab` `flake` `green-checkpoint` `vitest` `load` `mock-bleed` `timeout` `triage` `debugging` `admin-testing` | active | 2026-06-18 | When you're asked to 'get the Tests tab green', most reds you see under a loaded box (load avg 30–90, fleet + benchmarks running) are load-induced flakes that self-green, NOT product/test defects. Two flake classes dominate operator-core/operator-vite: (1) TIMEOUT-under-load — a heavy module-import-graph transform (or real PG/FS I/O) counted against a per-test timeout tips over 60s/15s under CPU starvation (a 43-line test can take 50s purely from transforming its `await import(...)` graph); (2) MODULE-REGISTRY-RACE — 'sql is not a function' / 'Cannot read properties of undefined' from a parallel mock-bleed between vi.mock-heavy files. A GENUINE red is deterministic (fails the same way in an isolated re-run) and PERSISTS as the file's latest row. Triage with testing:runs (latest-per-file) + an isolated re-run before touching anything. Also covers: the breadth-test-with-real-I/O anti-pattern, the green-checkpoint code-143 gate-stall, and why a manual `--reporter=dot` run never records a green row. |
| [A tree-scanning guard is blob-invariant under its own repair — why blob containment cannot settle its gate red](/internal/docs/agent-insights/tree-scanning-guards-are-blob-invariant-under-their-own-repair) | `release-gate` `testing` `containment` `false-negatives` `frozen-candidate` | active | 2026-09-22 | Blob containment against a frozen candidate is the standard way to tell a current gate red from a stale one — but it is informative only when the failing test's SUBJECT is reachable through that file's import graph. When the subject is the TREE (a scanner) or an ARTIFACT THE TEST SPAWNS (a shell script, a CLI), the repair lands somewhere containment never looks, so blob identity is the EXPECTED state and reads as 'unfixed'. This is what the gate cell renders as `fixInRepairHead: false` / `stillBrokenCount`. Two settlement methods, one per subject shape: feed COMMITTED blobs to a scanner's exported detector (with a mandatory positive control), or pin an external-artifact test's outcome-determining input set and run it once. Also records the staleness caveat that runs BACKWARDS for a scanner walking untracked files. |

| [A workspace domain resolving to a wrong IP in the su Bash tool is a LOCAL DNS hijack, not a sandbox feature — verify via an external path before ever reporting an outage](/internal/docs/agent-insights/su-box-dns-hijack-not-sandbox-synthesis) | `dns` `sandbox` `bash-tool` `su` `network` `false-positive` `outage` `cloudflare` `gotcha` `EI-12952` | active | 2026-07-18 | EI-12952: an su session diagnosed a total DNS/production outage of papercusp.com from inside its own Bash tool — dig/curl/python all agreed, and every resolver (1.1.1.1, 8.8.8.8, even the zone's own authoritative NS) 'confirmed' it. The domain was never down. Root cause verified live: this dev box's LAN silently HIJACKS plain UDP:53 answers for papercusp.com (and only papercusp.com — google.com/example.com/sqlite.org resolve correctly over the same path) to a stale/wrong IP, while TCP:53 and any externally-routed check (WebFetch) return the true, healthy Cloudflare-proxied answer. Never trust in-sandbox DNS for a workspace-owned domain as your only signal — verify externally before reporting an outage or touching DNS records. |

| [Absorbing-state guards, and why your detector didn't notice](/internal/docs/agent-insights/absorbing-state-guards-and-self-report-detectors) | `guards` `watchdogs` `detectors` `liveness` `failure-modes` `p2p` `git-sync` `replication` `design-smell` | active | 2026-07-25 | Six failure instances, four found independently on one day by four agents in different lanes, then recognised as one pattern. Five guard/detector shapes: (A) a guard whose baseline or latch advances ONLY through its own success cannot clear itself — the first failure is permanent; (B) when a component reports its own failure, a detector that infers the same condition from a downstream proxy instead is strictly weaker, because the proxy can be confounded; (C) a detector whose coverage is gated on the success it exists to detect the absence of reports healthy precisely when the thing has failed; (D) a correctly-written detector aimed at an EMPTY set reports a clean pass forever. The headline is the detectors, not the guards: the guard bugs cost hours, the detector bugs cost DAYS — and the Shape D instance had been blind for its entire life. |

| [Account pins silently route to `local`: the stale gateway pool](/internal/docs/agent-insights/inference-gateway-silent-local-fallback) | `inference-gateway` `account-routing` `account-pin` `psu` `local-fallback` `rate-limit` `failover` `detectability` | active | 2026-06-18 | Why `psu --account X` (or any cup account pin) can land all usage on the wrong account. The pin routes via the localhost inference gateway (:8788) + the x-papercusp-account header; the gateway resolves its account pool ONCE at startup and caches it for its whole lifetime. A swallowed pool-load error used to collapse that pool to a one-account `local` fallback (the box's ~/.claude login), so every pin was silently ignored for hours. How to spot it (/healthz accountId:local), the fix (fail-closed resolve + restart), and the x-papercusp-routed-account header that makes a pin's real destination visible. |
| [Across a submodule boundary, git answers a question you did not ask — and the answer is always the safe-sounding one](/internal/docs/agent-insights/verifying-a-change-across-a-submodule-boundary) | — | — | 2026-07-26 | The superproject stores a GITLINK, not submodule file content. So `git show <sha>:<submodule-path>/file` prints nothing, `git status --porcelain <submodule-path>/file` prints nothing, and `git ls-files` says the path is unknown to git — while the file exists, is committed, and is live. Every one of those empties reads as 'absent' or 'clean', and they compose into a confident false negative in the most expensive direction: 'my instrumentation is not in this build, abort the run.' Resolve the gitlink (git ls-tree) or ask dev:pipeline_position; never grep across the boundary. |

| [Agent-activity truth: a claim is not progress — who's-doing-what is a LIVE derived signal](/internal/docs/agent-insights/agent-activity-truth) | `coordination` `liveness` `claims` `progress` `stalled` `orphaned` `reconciler` `fleet-assignment` `watchdog` `who-is-doing-what` `EI-2311` `EI-6874` `EI-9696` `incident` | active | 2026-06-21 | The durable fix for 'agents are wrong about what other agents are doing'. A claim is a RESERVATION and a heartbeat proves the PROCESS is alive — NEITHER proves the WORK is advancing, and a coord broadcast outlives the work. The incident: a gate-fix cup DIED but its 'spawned for X' broadcast was read as 'actively worked' by the Mug + idle agents + the coordinator for ~1h, so the reds sat owned-but-unworked and blocked every deploy. The fix: a third signal (last_progress_at) + a reconciled fleet_assignment view (orphaned = dead holder, stalled = live-but-not-progressing) + the whoIsDoingWhat resolver + a flag-gated reconciler that auto-frees dead/stalled claims + immediate spawn-death release + the stalled-claim watchdog. Read the live signal, never the announcement. |

| [Background-task notifications name the WRAPPER or the WATCHER, not the WORK — a terminal-sounding message about the wrong subject (EI-21855015171700096)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/background-notification-names-wrapper-or-watcher-not-work) | `bash` `background-tasks` `notifications` `completion-integrity` `nohup` `watcher` `run_in_background` | active | 2026-08-30 | A run_in_background completion notification always reports on the LITERAL command you launched, not on whatever that command backgrounds further, and not on whatever it is watching. Two concrete traps: (1) `nohup /tmp/script.sh &` inside a run_in_background call reports "completed (exit code 0)" within seconds because the LAUNCHING shell exited — the nohup'd script is still on file 1 of 8. (2) A `until kill -0 <pid>; do sleep; done` watcher gets reaped by the harness mid-wait and reports "killed / was stopped" — which reads as "the job ended" but is the opposite: the watched job was alive and minutes from its own deadline. Distinct from the pipe/exit-code-lying trap (background-test-pipe-exit-code-trap) and from a task's own death under host overload (native-bash-background-task-killed-under-host-overload) — this is about the notification's SUBJECT being ambiguous, not its exit code being wrong. |
| [Bare "fetch failed" spawn failures = loopback retry swallowing undici UND_ERR_* codes during restarts](/internal/docs/agent-insights/bare-fetch-failed-spawns-are-loopback-undici-codes) | `spawn` `loopback` `undici` `fetch-failed` `watchdog` `EI-390` `network` `retry` `deploy` `debugging` | active | 2026-06-13 | A mug/spawn that fails with a contentless `fetch failed` (null output_tail, parent_role=operator) is NOT credential exhaustion (that classifies as 'auth') — it is the operator's loopbackFetch to its OWN invoke route failing during a deploy restart. loopbackFetch retries transient errors, but isTransientNetworkError early-returned false on undici's UND_ERR_SOCKET/UND_ERR_CONNECT_TIMEOUT codes (a server-closed socket mid-request), so no retry → bare 'fetch failed'. Fix: recognize UND_ERR_* connection codes + fall through to the message heuristic, and record the cause code via describeFetchError so the watchdog sample is self-diagnosing. |

| [Best-effort PG writes swallow cast errors — pin the rows, and know jsonb int casts ERROR on fractions](/internal/docs/agent-insights/best-effort-swallows-hide-pg-cast-errors) | `postgres` `jsonb` `cast` `best-effort` `scout` `lens-weights` `testing` `integration` | active | 2026-06-12 | jsonb_to_recordset(... AS x(col int)) raises 'invalid input syntax for type integer: "0.5"' instead of rounding — a fractional value in a payload silently kills the WHOLE statement. Inside a warn-and-continue try/catch (the scout lens-weight upsert pattern), that failure is invisible: tests pass on no-error, prod state just goes stale. Second occurrence of this exact class on the same seam (bytes.js Array serialization was the first). Rule: a best-effort write needs a test that asserts the ROWS LANDED, never just no-throw; and round/truncate explicitly before any jsonb int cast. |

| [capability:bash background jobs across calls — the ps aux red herring, and why a cup shouldn't drive a long-lived npm run dev this way (EI-8876)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/capability-bash-background-jobs-across-calls) | `capability` `sandbox` `bwrap` `unshare-pid` `background-jobs` `cup` `tauri` `agent-e2e` `EI-8876` `EI-8855` | active | 2026-07-09 | run_in_background:true jobs are tracked in the operator's own in-memory registry (bash-jobs.ts), NOT observable via a later capability:bash { ps aux } call — --unshare-pid gives every capability:bash invocation its OWN isolated PID namespace, so a follow-up ps aux can never see a prior call's sandboxed processes, sandbox or no sandbox, job alive or dead. That's expected, not evidence of non-persistence. The real risk for a genuinely long-lived job (npm run dev) on this shared multi-agent box is EI-8855's class: ANY agent's routine operator restart wipes every other agent's in-flight background-job registry — use capability:bash_output's stranded_by_operator_restart reason to tell the two apart, and prefer native (unsandboxed, un-shared-registry) Bash for a genuinely long-lived dev-server verification session. |

| [coding-pot `cup` (self-pulls a claim-spec) vs coding-factory `scoper` (gets a FEATURE_ID pushed) — don't conflate them](/internal/docs/agent-insights/coding-pot-cup-vs-coding-factory-scoper) | `coding-pot` `coding-factory` `cup` `scoper` `mug` `claim-spec` `scheduler` `get_next` `fleet-spawn` `blueprint` `push-vs-pull` `role-confusion` | superseded | 2026-06-23 | Papercusp has TWO distinct coding execution models that share the cup:spawn chokepoint, and agents repeatedly conflate their work units. The coding POT is a mug + self-pulling `cup`s: the Mug steers a cup with a versioned claim-spec (scheduler:set_claim_spec) and the cup PULLS its own next item via scheduler:get_next. The coding-FACTORY is the OPPOSITE — a PUSH pipeline (scoper→architect→worker→validator→documenter→curator, driven by a director's spine) where each role is HANDED a FEATURE_ID by the orchestrator (NEXT_SCOPER/NEXT_ARCHITECT/… all carry extras:["FEATURE_ID={feature}"]) and NEVER calls get_next. The exact observed failure: an agent spawned role="scoper" and set claim-specs on it to test the coding-pot handoff — but a scoper never self-pulls, so the claim-spec is INERT and (lacking a pushed FEATURE_ID) the scoper has nothing to do. For a claim-spec self-pulling worker the role is "cup", NEVER scoper. operator-spawn now REFUSES a factory pipeline role spawned with no pushed-feature context and points at role="cup"; set_claim_spec WARNS when its beeId resolves to a non-self-pulling role. |

| [COMMITTED does not mean SETTLED — git-sync splits one logical change across ticks, so a type red can belong to nobody](/internal/docs/agent-insights/committed-is-not-settled-git-sync-landing-race) | `lint-tsc` `git-sync` `type-errors` `shared-checkout` `triage` `false-positive` `gate` `work-items` `landing-race` `locks` `atomicity` `edit-cohort` | active | 2026-08-02 | The committed-vs-uncommitted test agents use to triage a new-file type red has a third population it cannot express. git-sync commits the WHOLE shared tree on a schedule, so ONE logical multi-file change routinely lands across TWO commits; in between, the committed tree holds a half-landed change that typechecks as a genuine error belonging to no one. Measured on WI-6764: a 7m49s window in which learning-slo.ts emitted a WatchdogSource union member that had not landed yet. An agent ran lint:tsc inside that window, correctly checked git status (clean), correctly concluded 'committed therefore standing red', and filed a major bug for a red that had healed itself ~1 minute before they wrote it up. Their reasoning was sound; the heuristic is incomplete. Also documents the AUTHOR's side: how to stop the split happening at all — claim the work-item before editing so your locks carry a goalRef, which is what lets git-sync's edit-cohort expansion keep a migration and its code in one commit. |

| [Coord-canary SLO breach: attribute per-step before optimizing — a slow INSERT ≠ a slow read](/internal/docs/agent-insights/coord-canary-breach-attribute-per-step) | `coordination` `canary` `performance` `postgres` `notify` `pg-notify` `substrate-outbox` `contention` `debugging` `root-cause` `EI-401` | active | 2026-06-13 | When the coord-probe-canary breaches its cycle SLO, read each step's latency before picking a culprit. EI-401 was 'fixed' by optimizing a read path (readAckedMsgIds full-table scan) — but the breach numbers showed the full-table READ (inbox, 477ms) was fine while a pure single-INSERT step (ping, 12.9s) and the ack INSERT (~9s) dominated. The probe carries harness_slug=NULL so its insert is minimal (one row + one pg_notify) — a minimal insert taking 12.9s is EXTERNAL contention: NOTIFY-queue commit serialization across the fleet + substrate_outbox capture cost + pool/IO pressure. Optimizing the read neither explains nor prevents a write-contention breach. |

| [Deciding "is this already built?" — four false-positive classes, and why precision must be reported PER LEG](/internal/docs/agent-insights/prior-art-screens-four-false-positive-classes) | `prior-art` `novelty` `scout` `ideas` `backlog` `triage` `code-search` `git-grep` `false-positive` `precision` `duplicate-work` `reuse-first` | active | 2026-08-03 | Any screen that searches the tree to decide whether an idea is already implemented — a tool, or an agent doing it by hand — hits four distinct false-positive classes, each of which produces a confident, well-formed WRONG answer. Measured on the real Scout backlog (WI-9476, 2026-08-03): (1) SELF-CONTAMINATION, the screen finding its own documentation of the ideas it screens; (2) DROPPED/DEPRECATED read as built, when both states close an item precisely because nobody built it; (3) an OPEN SIBLING cited as "existing" being duplication rather than prior art; (4) a SYMBOL existing mistaken for the MECHANISM existing. Fixing all four moved the drop rate 80% -> 45%. The larger lesson is methodological: a multi-leg screen's precision cannot be assessed from one leg's evidence, and doing so produced a retracted precision figure that nearly caused the strongest leg to be demoted. |

| [Deploys crash-loop :3070 on MODULE_NOT_FOUND — an in-place node_modules rebuild under the live operator](/internal/docs/agent-insights/release-node-modules-swap-crash-window) | `operator` `release-pipeline` `deploy` `node_modules` `MODULE_NOT_FOUND` `cluster` `crash-loop` `connection-refused` `setup-release-checkout` `boot-integrity` | active | 2026-06-25 | ':3070 connection refused on all pages' in recurring ~1–35 min windows: a deploy's setup-release-checkout.sh refreshed node_modules with `rm -rf $dest; cp -al` IN PLACE while the live operator kept serving from it, so for the ~minute the root copy took the tree was deleted/half-populated → the 32 cluster workers respawned mid-copy, hit MODULE_NOT_FOUND (@modelcontextprotocol/sdk, @papercusp/plugin-loader), exhausted the 160-respawn budget → 0 workers → connection refused, until the copy finished and it self-recovered. Root fix: atomic sibling-temp swap (two renames) so the live tree is only ever complete. Defense-in-depth: a workspace-resolution gate (step 3c, EI-2118) that aborts before restart if any workspace symlink is dangling, a boot-integrity preflight (fail fast + named), and a cluster boot-failure backstop (halt the respawn storm) so an inconsistent tree degrades to a fast, loud, self-correcting fail instead of an hour-long all-pages outage. |

| [Dynamic egress-proxy toggle (and: the opus-429 cause was NOT the egress IP — see the Claude Code system-block doc)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/gateway-cloudflare-per-ip-opus-throttle-egress-toggle) | `inference-gateway` `egress-ip` `egress-proxy-toggle` `cloudflare` `opus` `429` `red-herring` `correction` `admin-endpoint` | active | 2026-06-30 | CORRECTION / RED-HERRING WARNING: this doc originally diagnosed the Scout/opus 180s-timeout + gateway 429s as a Cloudflare per-IP EDGE throttle on the datacenter egress IPs. That was WRONG — disproven live 2026-06-30. The real root cause is the missing Claude Code identifier as the first `system` block on Max-OAuth requests — see max-oauth-first-system-block-must-be-claude-code-identity. The egress IPs were a red herring (box-IP-only did not fix it; a claude-CLI opus call from the same box IP worked). What REMAINS valid here is the gateway's DYNAMIC EGRESS-PROXY TOGGLE this work added: flip between the per-account datacenter proxy pool and box-IP-only at runtime, no restart — useful for genuine egress-IP work, NOT for opus 429s. |

| [Empty-string env vars defeat `??` defaults — docker-compose `${VAR:-}` ships "", not unset](/internal/docs/agent-insights/empty-string-env-defeats-nullish-defaults) | `env` `docker-compose` `nodejs` `nullish-coalescing` `config-parsing` `scout` `budget-gate` `prod-outage` `restart` `debugging` | active | 2026-07-17 | A compose line like `SCOUT_CONVERSATION_BUDGET_USD: ${SCOUT_CONVERSATION_BUDGET_USD:-}` injects an EMPTY STRING into the container when the host env doesn't define the var. In Node, `'' ?? 'default'` keeps '' (empty string is not nullish), so `Number(process.env.X ?? '1')` becomes Number('') → 0. In the Restart scout-service this made the conversation budget $0 and the gate `cost >= 0` rejected EVERY message ('hit budget: $0.0000 ≥ $0') — a total prod chat outage with a perfectly healthy service. Same class hit OPENAI_BASE_URL earlier (commit 4920dd05). Rule: any env consumed with a default must treat empty/whitespace/garbage/non-positive as unset — parse via a helper (see apps/scout-service/src/scout/budget.ts parseBudgetUsd), or use `:-realdefault` in compose, never `:-`. Diagnostic tell: an error that should depend on accumulated state firing on the FIRST request of a fresh session means the threshold parsed to zero, not that the meter is wrong. |

| [Green-checkpoint reaped at 25m — the executor-reaper no-output window must exceed the suite runtime](/internal/docs/agent-insights/green-checkpoint-reaped-by-executor-window) | `green-checkpoint` `dbos` `executor-reaper` `release-pipeline` `deploy` `idle-session-reaper-window` `main-frozen` `skipped-locked` `timing-invariant` | active | 2026-06-29 | The DBOS executor-reaper cancels any routineFire that has recorded no operation_output within DEFAULT_STALE_ROUTINE_FIRE_NO_OUTPUT_MS of its CREATION — a wall-clock cap, not a silence timer. The green-checkpoint runs its whole suite as one opaque `await runScript()` with no intermediate DBOS checkpoint, so whenever that window falls below the suite's runtime every long checkpoint is cancelled mid-suite and never produces a verdict, freezing `main` (every fleet deploy) for days. It has now happened twice — window 25m vs a 55m suite (2026-06-29, ~64h), then window 60m vs a 120m suite (WI-6112, 2026-07-26). The chain must stay monotonic: suite(120m) < self-watchdog(180m) < window(185m) < lock-stale(190m), now enforced by a test. |

| [Inference admission control loop — gateway priority tiers (sink) + Mug capacity dispatch (source)](/internal/docs/agent-insights/gateway-admission-control-loop) | `inference-gateway` `admission-control` `priority-tiers` `mug` `capacity-dispatch` `placement` `backpressure` `scarcity` `fleet` `runbook` | active | 2026-06-22 | Under inference scarcity the platform runs a TWO-SIDED admission control loop sharing ONE capacity oracle (the gateway /stats). SINK side (gateway-priority-tiers, GATEWAY_PRIORITY_TIERS): per-tier admission caps + a reserved tier-1 floor so the interactive/Mug/Scout lane is never crowded out by batch cups — it ALLOCATES scarce capacity fairly. SOURCE side (mug-capacity-dispatch, QUEEN_CAPACITY_DISPATCH): the Mug clamps fresh-cup placement to what the pool can sustain (read from the same gateway /stats) so it stops FLOODING a saturated pool with requests that just park/fail — it REDUCES the inflow. Neither CREATES capacity (the durable lever is more accounts); they make scarce capacity go to the right work. Both flag-gated, fail-safe (gateway-unreachable ⇒ no change), byte-identical off. |
